I’ll be doing something completely routine at work, like measuring a railing or checking something near a stairwell, and then I hear a ladder scrape or some metal shift, and suddenly I can see my husband falling. Like not just remembering it in a normal way, but like it’s right there in front of me for a second.\n\nAnd my body reacts before I even really understand what’s happening. My stomach just drops, and my hands get shaky, and I have to stop what I’m doing because it feels so real in that moment. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m supposed to be focused and professional, and instead I’m standing there trying to breathe and pretend I’m fine. It can come out of nowhere, and once it starts, it’s hard to push it back out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and images of your husband falling only happen when you're reminded of the event by something, like the sound of a ladder scraping, or do they also occur at other times without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when something reminds me of it, I think. Like, I’ll be measuring a railing or walking near a stairwell for work, and everything is normal, and then I hear something like a ladder scrape or metal shift, and it’s like my brain just… throws me right back into it.\n\nBut it feels so sudden that sometimes it seems like it comes out of nowhere, if that makes sense. I might not even realize what set it off until after. One second I’m just doing my job, and then I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops immediately, and my hands start getting shaky, and I have to stop and kind of collect myself.\n\nSo I guess there usually is some kind of reminder, but it can be small enough that I don’t notice it at first. It doesn’t feel like a regular memory. It feels like it intrudes on me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of fear and guilt?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now. Pretty much since my husband’s fall, when he was doing that porch repair. I keep thinking it should have eased up by now, because at first I told myself, you know, of course I’m shaken, it just happened. But it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nSo… yeah, about four months of this fear and guilt kind of hanging around, and the thoughts coming back on me. It feels like it started right then and just never fully stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I wanted to admit at first, honestly.\n\nWith work, I’ve started quietly turning down certain jobs if I know they involve raised porches or second-story decks. I’ll tell myself it’s because my schedule is too full or because someone else is closer to the site, but the real reason is that I just don’t want to be up there looking at railings or deck boards and picturing what happened. Even reading the description of the job sometimes makes my chest tighten, like I’m already bracing for it.\n\nAnd at home, I avoid that spot. I’ll go around a different way if I can, or I’ll keep myself busy in another part of the house. If anyone brings up the repair, I kind of shut it down or change the subject. I find excuses not to talk about it, not to look at the place where he fell, not to deal with anything connected to it. I know avoiding it probably makes it loom larger, but in the moment it feels like the only way to get through the day without falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a big dramatic way, like I’m cutting people off or anything, but I’m more short-tempered than I used to be.\n\nMy patience is just… thinner now. With my husband especially, which makes me feel terrible, because he’s the one who got hurt. If he asks me the same thing twice, or needs me to repeat something, I’ll snap back sharper than I mean to. It’ll come out with this edge in my voice, and then I’ll hear myself and immediately feel awful. I’ll think, why am I talking to him like that? He didn’t do anything wrong.\n\nAnd at work it happens too. If a contractor brushes off a safety issue or acts like I’m being picky, I get irritated really fast. I’ve always taken safety seriously, obviously, but now it feels personal in a way I can’t quite separate. So if someone says, “It’s fine, we’ve always done it this way,” I can feel myself getting hot, and I’ll be harsher than I probably need to be. I don’t yell or anything like that, but my tone gets hard, and I know people notice.\n\nSo, yes, it’s affected things. I’m not as easy to be around sometimes. And then afterward I feel guilty and try to smooth it over, but it’s embarrassing, because I know I’m reacting stronger than the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Yes… most nights, honestly. I’ll get into bed tired, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind just starts going. I lie there for an hour, sometimes two, replaying things over and over. It’s not even like I’m trying to think about it — it just shows up, and then I’m stuck with it.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel very solid. I wake up at little noises in the house, like every creak or shift makes me tense up. Sometimes I wake from dreams where I hear him hit the ground again, and it feels so real for a second that I’m just lying there with my heart going, trying to remind myself I’m in bed and it’s not happening right then.\n\nSo yes… sleep has been pretty bad. Not every single minute of every night, but most nights it’s hard to get settled and stay asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I mean, I still like the things I liked before, at least in my head. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them, or I feel distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy going out with friends or working on little house projects or watching a show with my husband?” I’d say yes, I think so. I haven’t completely stopped caring about those things. I still want to have normal evenings and normal weekends. It just sometimes takes more effort to actually settle into it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest. Not in the sense that everything feels pointless or I don’t care about anything anymore. It’s more uneven than that, I guess. Some days I’m less in the mood, but I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I do get jumpy sometimes if something is sudden, but I’m not sure it’s more than anyone else would be, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if someone drops a tool or a door slams unexpectedly, I’ll react, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly jumping out of my skin over every little sound. I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't care about things you used to care about since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds. I mean, I still care about the same things — my husband, our home, my work, my family, the little routines we had. I don’t feel like I suddenly became someone who just doesn’t care anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I’m tired or distracted, and maybe I don’t show up the same way I used to. Like I might not have the same energy to make plans or keep up with every little thing, and then I feel guilty because I wonder if it looks like I don’t care. But inside, I do. I still care a lot.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve felt numb to things I used to care about. Maybe a little worn down at times, but not like I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of your husband's fall when they come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice I’m doing it at first, because it feels like I’m just being practical or moving on to the next thing, but if I’m honest, I’ve been avoiding it.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started turning down certain jobs if I know they involve raised porches or second-story decks. I’ll tell myself it’s scheduling, or that someone else is better suited for it, or that I don’t want the hassle that day, but… it’s pretty clearly because it brings me right back to what happened. Even just seeing that kind of setup can make my mind go there, and I don’t want it to.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If my husband brings up the repair or something that needs to be done near the spot where he fell, I’ll kind of change the subject or find a reason to be in another room. I don’t really like looking at that area. I’ll walk around it, or keep busy with something else, or say we can talk about it later. It’s not that I think about it constantly on purpose — it’s more like when the memory starts to come up, I try to shut it down before it gets too clear.\n\nDoctor: Has your condition affected your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I’m still working, and I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job at all, because I can. I’m showing up, I’m getting things done. But it’s definitely changed the way I work.\n\nMy inspection reports take longer now than they used to, because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll write something up, then go back and check it again, and then wonder if I missed something or worded it wrong, and then I’ll look over the photos again. It’s frustrating because I used to be pretty confident and efficient with that part of the job.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down certain jobs, especially if they involve elevated decks or porches. Those used to be normal for me — I wouldn’t have thought twice about them. Now if I see one on the schedule, I start looking for reasons not to take it, or I’ll ask if someone else can handle it. I don’t love admitting that, because it feels unprofessional, but it’s true.\n\nWith relationships, I don’t think it’s been as obvious, but work has definitely been affected.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have significantly impacted your daily life or relationships in a way that's caused you distress or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it has, though maybe not in this dramatic way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still working, I’m still getting through my days, but it’s definitely causing problems for me.\n\nThe biggest thing is my job. My inspection reports take longer than they used to because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll finish one and then go back through it again, and then I’ll question whether I described something clearly enough or whether I missed a detail, and then I end up spending more time on it than I normally would. It makes me feel slower and less sure of myself, which is frustrating because I used to be pretty steady with that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down jobs with elevated decks or porches — the kind I used to take without even thinking about it. Now I see one come up and I start looking for a way out of it, or I ask if someone else can do it. That bothers me. It makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things moving.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life, mostly around work, and it does cause me distress because I don’t like feeling less capable than I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you no longer enjoy or look forward to things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about it in that exact way.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some big thing I used to love that I now completely don’t care about. It’s more like… I’ve been busy and kind of preoccupied, so I don’t always stop and notice whether I’m looking forward to things the same way. If something is on the calendar, I’ll usually still go or do it, and sometimes it’s fine once I’m there.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’d say I’ve “lost pleasure” in things, exactly. I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself participating less in hobbies or activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI mean, I’m trying to think of hobbies, specifically. I don’t have this big list of activities that I suddenly stopped doing. My schedule can get kind of full with work, and sometimes when I’m done for the day I’m just ready to be home and not have a lot planned, but I don’t know that that’s new or that I’d connect it to anything in particular.\n\nIf friends invite me to something, I’ll usually still consider it, and if it works with my schedule I might go. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m avoiding all the things I used to enjoy,” or anything like that. It’s possible I’m a little less enthusiastic sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a real drop in participating in hobbies or normal activities.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My stomach just drops, and my hands get shaky, and I have to stop what I’m doing because it feels so real in that moment. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m supposed to be focused and professional, and instead I’m standing there trying to breathe and pretend I’m fine. It can come out of nowhere, and once it starts, it’s hard to push it back out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and images of your husband falling only happen when you're reminded of the event by something, like the sound of a ladder scraping, or do they also occur at other times without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when something reminds me of it, I think. Like, I’ll be measuring a railing or walking near a stairwell for work, and everything is normal, and then I hear something like a ladder scrape or metal shift, and it’s like my brain just… throws me right back into it.\n\nBut it feels so sudden that sometimes it seems like it comes out of nowhere, if that makes sense. I might not even realize what set it off until after. One second I’m just doing my job, and then I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops immediately, and my hands start getting shaky, and I have to stop and kind of collect myself.\n\nSo I guess there usually is some kind of reminder, but it can be small enough that I don’t notice it at first. It doesn’t feel like a regular memory. It feels like it intrudes on me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of fear and guilt?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now. Pretty much since my husband’s fall, when he was doing that porch repair. I keep thinking it should have eased up by now, because at first I told myself, you know, of course I’m shaken, it just happened. But it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nSo… yeah, about four months of this fear and guilt kind of hanging around, and the thoughts coming back on me. It feels like it started right then and just never fully stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I wanted to admit at first, honestly.\n\nWith work, I’ve started quietly turning down certain jobs if I know they involve raised porches or second-story decks. I’ll tell myself it’s because my schedule is too full or because someone else is closer to the site, but the real reason is that I just don’t want to be up there looking at railings or deck boards and picturing what happened. Even reading the description of the job sometimes makes my chest tighten, like I’m already bracing for it.\n\nAnd at home, I avoid that spot. I’ll go around a different way if I can, or I’ll keep myself busy in another part of the house. If anyone brings up the repair, I kind of shut it down or change the subject. I find excuses not to talk about it, not to look at the place where he fell, not to deal with anything connected to it. I know avoiding it probably makes it loom larger, but in the moment it feels like the only way to get through the day without falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a big dramatic way, like I’m cutting people off or anything, but I’m more short-tempered than I used to be.\n\nMy patience is just… thinner now. With my husband especially, which makes me feel terrible, because he’s the one who got hurt. If he asks me the same thing twice, or needs me to repeat something, I’ll snap back sharper than I mean to. It’ll come out with this edge in my voice, and then I’ll hear myself and immediately feel awful. I’ll think, why am I talking to him like that? He didn’t do anything wrong.\n\nAnd at work it happens too. If a contractor brushes off a safety issue or acts like I’m being picky, I get irritated really fast. I’ve always taken safety seriously, obviously, but now it feels personal in a way I can’t quite separate. So if someone says, “It’s fine, we’ve always done it this way,” I can feel myself getting hot, and I’ll be harsher than I probably need to be. I don’t yell or anything like that, but my tone gets hard, and I know people notice.\n\nSo, yes, it’s affected things. I’m not as easy to be around sometimes. And then afterward I feel guilty and try to smooth it over, but it’s embarrassing, because I know I’m reacting stronger than the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Yes… most nights, honestly. I’ll get into bed tired, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind just starts going. I lie there for an hour, sometimes two, replaying things over and over. It’s not even like I’m trying to think about it — it just shows up, and then I’m stuck with it.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel very solid. I wake up at little noises in the house, like every creak or shift makes me tense up. Sometimes I wake from dreams where I hear him hit the ground again, and it feels so real for a second that I’m just lying there with my heart going, trying to remind myself I’m in bed and it’s not happening right then.\n\nSo yes… sleep has been pretty bad. Not every single minute of every night, but most nights it’s hard to get settled and stay asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I mean, I still like the things I liked before, at least in my head. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them, or I feel distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy going out with friends or working on little house projects or watching a show with my husband?” I’d say yes, I think so. I haven’t completely stopped caring about those things. I still want to have normal evenings and normal weekends. It just sometimes takes more effort to actually settle into it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest. Not in the sense that everything feels pointless or I don’t care about anything anymore. It’s more uneven than that, I guess. Some days I’m less in the mood, but I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I do get jumpy sometimes if something is sudden, but I’m not sure it’s more than anyone else would be, if that makes sense.\n\nLike if someone drops a tool or a door slams unexpectedly, I’ll react, but I wouldn’t say I’m constantly jumping out of my skin over every little sound. I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern. It’s not one of the main things I’d point to, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don't care about things you used to care about since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds. I mean, I still care about the same things — my husband, our home, my work, my family, the little routines we had. I don’t feel like I suddenly became someone who just doesn’t care anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I’m tired or distracted, and maybe I don’t show up the same way I used to. Like I might not have the same energy to make plans or keep up with every little thing, and then I feel guilty because I wonder if it looks like I don’t care. But inside, I do. I still care a lot.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve felt numb to things I used to care about. Maybe a little worn down at times, but not like I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of your husband's fall when they come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. I don’t always notice I’m doing it at first, because it feels like I’m just being practical or moving on to the next thing, but if I’m honest, I’ve been avoiding it.\n\nAt work, for example, I’ve started turning down certain jobs if I know they involve raised porches or second-story decks. I’ll tell myself it’s scheduling, or that someone else is better suited for it, or that I don’t want the hassle that day, but… it’s pretty clearly because it brings me right back to what happened. Even just seeing that kind of setup can make my mind go there, and I don’t want it to.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If my husband brings up the repair or something that needs to be done near the spot where he fell, I’ll kind of change the subject or find a reason to be in another room. I don’t really like looking at that area. I’ll walk around it, or keep busy with something else, or say we can talk about it later. It’s not that I think about it constantly on purpose — it’s more like when the memory starts to come up, I try to shut it down before it gets too clear.\n\nDoctor: Has your condition affected your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I’m still working, and I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job at all, because I can. I’m showing up, I’m getting things done. But it’s definitely changed the way I work.\n\nMy inspection reports take longer now than they used to, because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll write something up, then go back and check it again, and then wonder if I missed something or worded it wrong, and then I’ll look over the photos again. It’s frustrating because I used to be pretty confident and efficient with that part of the job.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down certain jobs, especially if they involve elevated decks or porches. Those used to be normal for me — I wouldn’t have thought twice about them. Now if I see one on the schedule, I start looking for reasons not to take it, or I’ll ask if someone else can handle it. I don’t love admitting that, because it feels unprofessional, but it’s true.\n\nWith relationships, I don’t think it’s been as obvious, but work has definitely been affected.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have significantly impacted your daily life or relationships in a way that's caused you distress or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it has, though maybe not in this dramatic way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still working, I’m still getting through my days, but it’s definitely causing problems for me.\n\nThe biggest thing is my job. My inspection reports take longer than they used to because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll finish one and then go back through it again, and then I’ll question whether I described something clearly enough or whether I missed a detail, and then I end up spending more time on it than I normally would. It makes me feel slower and less sure of myself, which is frustrating because I used to be pretty steady with that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down jobs with elevated decks or porches — the kind I used to take without even thinking about it. Now I see one come up and I start looking for a way out of it, or I ask if someone else can do it. That bothers me. It makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things moving.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life, mostly around work, and it does cause me distress because I don’t like feeling less capable than I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you no longer enjoy or look forward to things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about it in that exact way.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some big thing I used to love that I now completely don’t care about. It’s more like… I’ve been busy and kind of preoccupied, so I don’t always stop and notice whether I’m looking forward to things the same way. If something is on the calendar, I’ll usually still go or do it, and sometimes it’s fine once I’m there.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’d say I’ve “lost pleasure” in things, exactly. I haven’t really noticed that as one of the main problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself participating less in hobbies or activities that you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way, at least.\n\nI mean, I’m trying to think of hobbies, specifically. I don’t have this big list of activities that I suddenly stopped doing. My schedule can get kind of full with work, and sometimes when I’m done for the day I’m just ready to be home and not have a lot planned, but I don’t know that that’s new or that I’d connect it to anything in particular.\n\nIf friends invite me to something, I’ll usually still consider it, and if it works with my schedule I might go. I haven’t really sat down and thought, “Oh, I’m avoiding all the things I used to enjoy,” or anything like that. It’s possible I’m a little less enthusiastic sometimes, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a real drop in participating in hobbies or normal activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself pulling back from social activities or things you usually do for fun since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. I mean, I’ve thought about it a little since you asked the last couple questions, and I don’t think I’m really pulling back from people in a clear way.\n\nLike, if there’s something planned or someone asks me to do something, I don’t automatically avoid it. I might be tired after work or not feel especially motivated sometimes, but that doesn’t feel totally new to me, and I’m not sure I’d connect it directly to my husband’s fall. I still do normal things if they come up.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me as a change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

---------- turn 17 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F43.1\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I just can’t shake this feeling I’ve had. It’s like this heavy mix of fear and guilt that sits on me all day. I feel tense, like I’m constantly waiting for something else to go wrong, even when nothing is happening right then.\n\nAnd I keep blaming myself, going over it in my head like I should’ve done something differently, like I should’ve stopped what happened somehow. I know that might not sound completely rational, but it feels real, and it’s been hard to just function normally with that sitting there all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same thoughts or images that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, that happens. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it, it just kind of barges in. I’ll be doing something completely routine at work, like measuring a railing or checking something near a stairwell, and then I hear a ladder scrape or some metal shift, and suddenly I can see my husband falling. Like not just remembering it in a normal way, but like it’s right there in front of me for a second.\n\nAnd my body reacts before I even really understand what’s happening. My stomach just drops, and my hands get shaky, and I have to stop what I’m doing because it feels so real in that moment. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m supposed to be focused and professional, and instead I’m standing there trying to breathe and pretend I’m fine. It can come out of nowhere, and once it starts, it’s hard to push it back out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and images of your husband falling only happen when you're reminded of the event by something, like the sound of a ladder scraping, or do they also occur at other times without any obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly when something reminds me of it, I think. Like, I’ll be measuring a railing or walking near a stairwell for work, and everything is normal, and then I hear something like a ladder scrape or metal shift, and it’s like my brain just… throws me right back into it.\n\nBut it feels so sudden that sometimes it seems like it comes out of nowhere, if that makes sense. I might not even realize what set it off until after. One second I’m just doing my job, and then I can see my husband falling like it’s happening right in front of me again. My stomach drops immediately, and my hands start getting shaky, and I have to stop and kind of collect myself.\n\nSo I guess there usually is some kind of reminder, but it can be small enough that I don’t notice it at first. It doesn’t feel like a regular memory. It feels like it intrudes on me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of fear and guilt?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months now. Pretty much since my husband’s fall, when he was doing that porch repair. I keep thinking it should have eased up by now, because at first I told myself, you know, of course I’m shaken, it just happened. But it hasn’t really gone away.\n\nSo… yeah, about four months of this fear and guilt kind of hanging around, and the thoughts coming back on me. It feels like it started right then and just never fully stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I’ll go around a different way if I can, or I’ll keep myself busy in another part of the house. If anyone brings up the repair, I kind of shut it down or change the subject. I find excuses not to talk about it, not to look at the place where he fell, not to deal with anything connected to it. I know avoiding it probably makes it loom larger, but in the moment it feels like the only way to get through the day without falling apart.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends since your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not in a big dramatic way, like I’m cutting people off or anything, but I’m more short-tempered than I used to be.\n\nMy patience is just… thinner now. With my husband especially, which makes me feel terrible, because he’s the one who got hurt. If he asks me the same thing twice, or needs me to repeat something, I’ll snap back sharper than I mean to. It’ll come out with this edge in my voice, and then I’ll hear myself and immediately feel awful. I’ll think, why am I talking to him like that? He didn’t do anything wrong.\n\nAnd at work it happens too. If a contractor brushes off a safety issue or acts like I’m being picky, I get irritated really fast. I’ve always taken safety seriously, obviously, but now it feels personal in a way I can’t quite separate. So if someone says, “It’s fine, we’ve always done it this way,” I can feel myself getting hot, and I’ll be harsher than I probably need to be. I don’t yell or anything like that, but my tone gets hard, and I know people notice.\n\nSo, yes, it’s affected things. I’m not as easy to be around sometimes. And then afterward I feel guilty and try to smooth it over, but it’s embarrassing, because I know I’m reacting stronger than the situation calls for.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: Yes… most nights, honestly. I’ll get into bed tired, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind just starts going. I lie there for an hour, sometimes two, replaying things over and over. It’s not even like I’m trying to think about it — it just shows up, and then I’m stuck with it.\n\nAnd when I finally do fall asleep, it doesn’t feel very solid. I wake up at little noises in the house, like every creak or shift makes me tense up. Sometimes I wake from dreams where I hear him hit the ground again, and it feels so real for a second that I’m just lying there with my heart going, trying to remind myself I’m in bed and it’s not happening right then.\n\nSo yes… sleep has been pretty bad. Not every single minute of every night, but most nights it’s hard to get settled and stay asleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I mean, I still like the things I liked before, at least in my head. It’s more that I don’t always get around to them, or I feel distracted when I’m doing them.\n\nLike, if you asked me, “Do you still enjoy going out with friends or working on little house projects or watching a show with my husband?” I’d say yes, I think so. I haven’t completely stopped caring about those things. I still want to have normal evenings and normal weekends. It just sometimes takes more effort to actually settle into it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a big loss of interest. Not in the sense that everything feels pointless or I don’t care about anything anymore. It’s more uneven than that, I guess. Some days I’m less in the mood, but I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things altogether.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “often,” no. I haven’t really noticed that as a big pattern. Like I might not have the same energy to make plans or keep up with every little thing, and then I feel guilty because I wonder if it looks like I don’t care. But inside, I do. I still care a lot.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve felt numb to things I used to care about. Maybe a little worn down at times, but not like I stopped caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thoughts and memories of your husband's fall when they come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Even just seeing that kind of setup can make my mind go there, and I don’t want it to.\n\nAnd at home it’s similar. If my husband brings up the repair or something that needs to be done near the spot where he fell, I’ll kind of change the subject or find a reason to be in another room. I don’t really like looking at that area. I’ll walk around it, or keep busy with something else, or say we can talk about it later. It’s not that I think about it constantly on purpose — it’s more like when the memory starts to come up, I try to shut it down before it gets too clear.\n\nDoctor: Has your condition affected your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. I mean, I’m still working, and I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do my job at all, because I can. I’m showing up, I’m getting things done. But it’s definitely changed the way I work.\n\nMy inspection reports take longer now than they used to, because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll write something up, then go back and check it again, and then wonder if I missed something or worded it wrong, and then I’ll look over the photos again. It’s frustrating because I used to be pretty confident and efficient with that part of the job.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down certain jobs, especially if they involve elevated decks or porches. Those used to be normal for me — I wouldn’t have thought twice about them. Now if I see one on the schedule, I start looking for reasons not to take it, or I’ll ask if someone else can handle it. I don’t love admitting that, because it feels unprofessional, but it’s true.\n\nWith relationships, I don’t think it’s been as obvious, but work has definitely been affected.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have significantly impacted your daily life or relationships in a way that's caused you distress or problems?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it has, though maybe not in this dramatic way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still working, I’m still getting through my days, but it’s definitely causing problems for me.\n\nThe biggest thing is my job. My inspection reports take longer than they used to because I keep second-guessing myself. I’ll finish one and then go back through it again, and then I’ll question whether I described something clearly enough or whether I missed a detail, and then I end up spending more time on it than I normally would. It makes me feel slower and less sure of myself, which is frustrating because I used to be pretty steady with that.\n\nAnd I’ve been turning down jobs with elevated decks or porches — the kind I used to take without even thinking about it. Now I see one come up and I start looking for a way out of it, or I ask if someone else can do it. That bothers me. It makes me feel like I’m not doing my job the way I should be, even though I’m still showing up and trying to keep things moving.\n\nSo yes, it’s impacted my daily life, mostly around work, and it does cause me distress because I don’t like feeling less capable than I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you no longer enjoy or look forward to things you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. If something is on the calendar, I’ll usually still go or do it, and sometimes it’s fine once I’m there.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’d say I’ve “lost pleasure” in things, exactly. I don’t have this big list of activities that I suddenly stopped doing. My schedule can get kind of full with work, and sometimes when I’m done for the day I’m just ready to be home and not have a lot planned, but I don’t know that that’s new or that I’d connect it to anything in particular.\n\nIf friends invite me to something, I’ll usually still consider it, and if it works with my schedule I might go. I might be tired after work or not feel especially motivated sometimes, but that doesn’t feel totally new to me, and I’m not sure I’d connect it directly to my husband’s fall. I still do normal things if they come up.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, not really. Not in a way that stands out to me as a change.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a significant decrease in interest in activities or hobbies that you previously enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not something I’d call significant.\n\nI’m trying to answer it carefully because I don’t want to say “no” too quickly if there’s some small change I’m not noticing. But when I think about things I used to enjoy, I don’t feel like there’s this obvious drop-off where I stopped caring about them or don’t want to do them anymore.\n\nSome days I’m more tired or I don’t feel like making plans, but that feels more like normal life stuff than a big loss of interest. If something comes up that I normally like, I can still usually enjoy it once I’m doing it. So I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed a significant decrease, no."
